Complete Compositional Syntax for Finite Transducers on Finite and Bi-Infinite Words
Titouan Carette, Marc de Visme, Vivien Ducros, Victor Lutfalla, Etienne Moutot

Abstract
Minimizing finite automata, proving trace equivalence of labelled transition systems or representing sofic subshifts involve very similar arguments, which suggests the possibility of a unified formalism. We propose finite states non-deterministic transducer as a lingua franca for automata theory, transition systems, and sofic subshifts. We introduce a compositional diagrammatical syntax for transducers in form of string diagrams interpreted as relations. This syntax comes with sound rewriting rules allowing diagrammatical reasoning. Our main result is the completeness of our equational theory, ensuring that language-equivalence, trace-equivalence, or subshift equivalence can always be proved using our rewriting rules.



1
Introduction

The interest of category theory for computer science is to provide an abstract language suitable to describe computational structures in their most general form, allowing to clarify connections between similar constructions appearing in different fields, sometimes seemingly unrelated.
One of those abstract ideas, ubiquitous in computer science, is a machine interacting with its environment by updating an internal set of states.
Most computational models fit this description, the paradigmatic example being Turing machines.
Such machines interact with their environment via inputs and outputs, thus their behavior can be described as the set of all possible input/output pairs, which are interpreted as a relation between a (possibly infinite) sequence of input events and another sequence of output events.
When constraining the set of internal states of a machine to be finite, one enforces interesting mathematical properties on the set of admissible behaviors, as they must be definable by finite means, in a certain way.

Let us take three examples of models exhibiting this kind of behavior.
The most famous is the finite automaton
[
Sip97
,
HMU07
]
, which only takes finite sequences of input events and evaluates if those sequences are valid or not.
The behavior then coincides with the language recognized by the automaton, and the possible behaviors are exactly the regular languages.
Perhaps more aligned with the interacting machine point of view, a Labelled Transition System (LTS) performs a transition (depending on an input event and its current state) that can produce an output event. In the LTS literature, we usually talk about the trace of the system to design its behavior.
The last model we will mention is perhaps less known by the computer science community and comes from dynamical systems.
A discrete-time dynamical system is a set
X
equipped with an update function
f:X\to X
implementing the dynamic.
Symbolic dynamics is the study of coarse-grained dynamical systems when
X
is partitioned into subsets indexed by finitely many symbols
[
LM21
]
.
One can learn a lot on the original dynamical system by studying the sequences of symbols induced by the evolution
f
.
Those sequences correspond to our general notion of behavior, and the ones ”definable by finite means” correspond to a set of sequences known as sofic subshifts.
The fact that similar mathematical methods, mostly involving regular languages, can be employed to handle those three cases is folklore.
However, to our knowledge, no framework exists to formally unify those three models.

We propose finite relational transducers as general objects subsuming automata, LTS, and symbolic dynamical systems.
The originality of our approach is to embrace a fully relational point of view, considering relations and not functions as more fundamental.
In other words, we will consider non-determinism to be more natural, and see determinism as an interesting special case.
This stance will lead us to present generalizations of well-known models that might feel unfamiliar, typically in the case of non-deterministic symbolic dynamical systems.
Still, we believe that the relational setting is the right place to understand clearly how the various models we present are linked.
Furthermore, the relational point of view is also the most well-suited for the use of string diagrams.

Indeed, our approach fits in a recent thread of research aiming to represent and reason on computational processes using diagrams with inputs and outputs.
Those diagrams can be thought of as boolean circuits-like structures, built from elementary generators (or gates) on which we can perform local rewritings by replacing a sub-circuit with another one having the same type and an equivalent behavior. Such methods have been successfully applied to provide compositional syntax for control flow graphs
[
BSZ14
,
BSZ21
]
or quantum computing
[
Wet20
]
for example.
As with the choice of moving from functions to relations, the diagrammatic paradigm requires the introduction of unfamiliar notions (for a reader used to automata, transition systems, or symbolic dynamic) from category theory
[
Sel11
]
.
Still, even if dressed in the unusual language of category theory and diagrams, the fundamental notions involved are the same.

We hope that the benefits of this choice outweigh the price paid for it.
Indeed, our formalism being formulated in the language of string diagrams (or equivalently, in the language of symmetric monoidal categories
[
Mac98
]
), we expect to be able to obtain straightforward generalizations by changing our base category (the mathematical world in which we interpret our diagrams) from relation to stochastic kernels or quantum channels.
This provides solid theoretical bases to define meaningful notions of automata, transition systems, and symbolic dynamical systems in the probabilistic and quantum case, on which we could develop similar simulation-based proof techniques.

One can see the current paper as the first step toward this goal. The use of diagrammatic formalism to unify notions of finite tilings and quantum tensors has been developed in
[
CM24
]
.

We hope our unified formalism can ease the transfer of ideas between the transition system and symbolic dynamics communities and stimulate the emergence of new questions and insights set off by our diagrammatical approach.

In this paper, we introduce relational transducers with finite states and show how they generalize finite automata, LTS, and symbolic dynamical systems. We define their behaviors on both finite and bi-infinite words.
In both cases, we fully characterize the admissible behaviors with the notion of regular and sofic relations, respectively.
We also introduce two string diagrammatic equational theories which are able to represent transducers on finite and bi-infinite words in a compositional way.
We show that those equational theories are expressive enough to represent any transducers and present two sound simulation principles that are strong enough to equate in a diagrammatic form any two transducers having the same behavior, both in the finite and bi-infinite case.
On a more technical side, we present a new diagrammatical formulation of the notion of backward and forward simulation and introduce a new notion of normal form for the presentation of a sofic subshift.

We start by introducing the diagrammatic aspects of our approach in
Section

2
.
In
Section

3
, with the notion of transducers used throughout the paper, we introduce our diagrammatical equational theory and completeness result for transducers acting on finite words.

Section

4
gathers results on sofic relations culminating in the extension of our diagrammatical theory and a completeness proof for the case of bi-infinite words.
Most of the proofs are postponed to the appendix.

2
Relations and their Diagrams

Before introducing transducers, we quickly present the formalism we use to handle relations between finite sets. We later use the same formalism for relations between infinite sets, more specifically sets of finite words and sets of bi-infinite words.
In the context of relations, those infinite sets will always be clearly denoted with a
\_^{*}
or a
\_^{\mathbb{Z}}
, as such, the letters
A,B,C,\dots
will refer to finite sets.

Given some finite sets
A_{1},\dots,A_{n},B_{1},\dots,B_{m}
, a relation
{\mathcal{R}}
from
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
to
\Pi_{j=1}^{m}B_{j}
is simply a subset
{\mathcal{R}}\subseteq\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\times\Pi_{j=1}^{m}B_{j}
. We write
a\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ b
, and say that
{\mathcal{R}}
relates
a
to
b
, whenever
(a,b)\in{\mathcal{R}}
. We represent such relation as the diagram on the left, the identity relation on
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
as the one on the middle, and the transposed of
{\mathcal{R}}
(defined by
b{\mathcal{R}}^{t}a
iff
a{\mathcal{R}}b
) as the one on the right.

\gamma_{A,B}=\{((a,b),(b,a))\mid a\in A,b\in B\}

\epsilon_{A}=\{((a,a),())\mid a\in A\}:A\times A\to{\mathds{1}}

\bullet_{A,B}=A\times B:A\to B
.

The general case is the partial composition. For example, given two relations
{\mathcal{R}}:A\to B\times C
and
{\mathcal{S}}:C\times D\to E
, their composite relation
\{((a,d),(b,e))\mid\exists c,a\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ (b,c),(c,d)\penalty\ {\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ e\}:A\times D\to B\times E
is written diagrammatically as shown on the right. Conversely, whenever we have a diagram that decomposes
{\mathcal{R}}:\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\to\Pi_{j=1}^{m}B_{j}
, into the partial composition of
{\mathcal{R}}_{1},\dots,{\mathcal{R}}_{r}
, and if we write
C_{1},\dots,C_{p}
for all the sets labeling the “internal wires” of the diagram, then we can build a logical formula equivalent to
(a_{1},\dots,a_{n})\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ (b_{1},\dots,b_{m})
of the following form:

\exists c_{1}\in C_{1},\dots,\exists c_{p}\in C_{p},(\_{\mathcal{R}}_{1}\_)\land\dots\land(\_{\mathcal{R}}_{r}\_)

where the blanks
\_

a_{i}
,
b_{j}
and
c_{k}

We remark that if any of the
{\mathcal{R}}_{\ell}
is the full relation
\bullet_{A,B}
, then it can safely be omitted from the logical formula as
(a\penalty\ \bullet_{A,B}\penalty\ b)
is always true.

Combining the partial composition with the swap, cup and cap defined above, one can reorganize the inputs and outputs of a relation, turning for example a relation
{\mathcal{R}}:A\times B\times C\to D
into a relation from
B\times A
to
D\times C
as on the right.

All those diagrams can actually be formalized using string diagrams from category theory
[
Mac98
]
. Indeed, finite sets and relations form a strict symmetric monoidal category, called
FinRel
. We recall the definition of such a category in
Appendix

A
, though for the sake of this paper, the only required understanding is ‘‘the diagrams works as intended, there is never a need to explicitly add bracketing, and all ’reasonable’ ways of rewriting a diagram yield the same relation’’. If we account for the cup and cap, relations even form a compact closed category
1
1

1

Where the dual is the transposition on relations, and the identity on objects.
, which exactly means that it satisfies the equations listed in
Figure

1
. We redirect to
[
Sel11
]
for a survey of variations around monoidal categories and the corresponding diagrammatic notations.

3
Transducers on Finite Words

3.1
Transducers and Regular Relations

For any finite set
A
, called an alphabet, we denote
A^{*}
the set of finite words over
A
, that is
A^{*}=\biguplus_{k\geq 0}A^{k}
. We write a word
w=w_{1}\dots w_{k}\in A^{*}
,
|w|
for its size
k
and
\epsilon
for the empty word.
The concatenation of two words
u
and
v
, respectively of size
n
and
m
, is denoted
uv
and has size
n+m
.
A subset of
A^{*}
is called a language over the alphabet
A
. A
uniform relation
between two alphabets
A
and
B
, denoted
\mathcal{R}:A^{*}\to B^{*}
, is a language over the product alphabet
A\times B
, equivalently, it’s a relation between
A^{*}
and
B^{*}
only relating words of the same size,
i.e.

u\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ v
implies
|u|=|v|
.

The product of two uniform relations
A^{*}\to B^{*}
and
C^{*}\to D^{*}
is formally a relation
A^{*}\times C^{*}\to B^{*}\times D^{*}
but to maintain the uniformity constraints, we choose to restrict it to a uniform relation
(A\times C)^{*}\to(B\times D)^{*}
.
While of course
(A\times B)^{*}\neq A^{*}\times B^{*}
as sets, we will identify the two as long as all relations involved are uniform. In other words, uniform relations are ignoring the elements of
A^{*}\times B^{*}
that are not in
(A\times B)^{*}
. We keep the notation
{\mathcal{R}}\times{\mathcal{S}}
for this uniform product, even if it is no longer a Cartesian product. Uniform relations are preserved by composition and (uniform) product, and include identities, as well as cups and caps. Similarly to
FinRel
, they form a compact closed strict symmetric monoidal
2
2

2

Note that the monoidal unit of
UniRel
is
{\mathds{1}}^{*}
, that is the words over the singleton alphabet, which is isomorphic to
\mathbb{N}
.
category called
UniRel
, meaning we can use the exact same graphical representation for them.

Definition 3.1

(Transducer)
.

A (non-deterministic finite) transducer is a tuple
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,I,F)
where
A
is a finite set representing the
input alphabet
,
B
is a finite set representing the
output alphabet
,
Q
is a finite set representing the
states
,
{\mathcal{T}}:A\times Q\to B\times Q
the
transition relation
,
{I}\subseteq Q
the
initial states
and
{F}\subseteq Q
the
final states
.

Similarly to non-deterministic finite automata, we write
q\xrightarrow[b]{a}q^{\prime}
whenever
((a,q),(b,q^{\prime}))
is a transition of
{\mathcal{T}}
. A
run
of length
k
within a transducer is a sequence

q_{0}\xrightarrow[b_{1}]{a_{1}}q_{1}\xrightarrow[b_{2}]{a_{2}}\dots\xrightarrow[b_{k}]{a_{k}}q_{k}

such that
q_{0}\in I
and
q_{k}\in F
. When such a run exists, we say that the transducer can transform the word
a_{1}\dots a_{k}
into
b_{1}\dots b_{k}
. The
behavior
of the transducer, written
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,I,F)
, is the uniform relation from
A^{*}
to
B^{*}
which relates
w
to
v
whenever the transducer can transform
w
into
v
.

A transducer with only inputs, said otherwise
B={\mathds{1}}
, is exactly a non-determinisitic finite automaton. Alternatively, every transducer from
A
to
B
can be seen as a non-deterministic finite automaton over
A\times B
. We recall that a
regular language
, is a language recognized by a finite automaton.
A
regular relation
is a uniform relation
A^{*}\to B^{*}
which is a regular language when seen as a subset of
(A\times B)^{*}
, which is the case if and only if it is the behavior of a transducer.

Proposition 3.1
.

Regular relations are preserved by composition and product. We call
RegRel
the subcategory of
UniRel
of regular relations.

The proof is based on standard construction of products and composition of transducers.

Any relation
\mathcal{R}:A\to B
can be lifted to a regular
3
3

3

The corresponding transducer has only one state which is accepting and initial and its transition relation is directly
\mathcal{R}
seen as having type
A\times{\mathds{1}}\to B\times{\mathds{1}}
.
relation
\mathcal{R}^{*}:A^{*}\to B^{*}
defined letter by letter:
\mathcal{R}^{*}=\{(u,v)\mid|u|=|v|,\forall 1\leq i\leq|u|,(u_{i},v_{i})\in{\mathcal{R}}\}
. Categorically, this lift is a faithful strong symmetric monoidal functor from
FinRel
to
RegRel
, which means:

Functoriality

({\mathcal{S}}\circ{\mathcal{R}})^{*}={\mathcal{S}}^{*}\circ{\mathcal{R}}^{*}
and
(\textbf{{id}}_{A})^{*}=\textbf{{id}}_{A^{*}}
.

Strong Monoidality

({\mathcal{R}}\times{\mathcal{S}})^{*}
can be identified with
{\mathcal{R}}^{*}\times{\mathcal{S}}^{*}
within
RegRel
.

Symmetric

(\gamma_{A,B})^{*}=\gamma_{A^{*},B^{*}}
.

Faithfullness

If
{\mathcal{R}}^{*}={\mathcal{S}}^{*}
, then necessarily
{\mathcal{R}}={\mathcal{S}}
.

This notion of transducers and regular relations appeared early in the history of theoretical computer science, one can find similar definitions, though in a different form in
[
EM65
]
.

3.2
Diagrammatic Representation of Transducers

Looking at
{\mathcal{R}}:\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}\to\Pi_{j=1}^{m}B_{j}
, we can see its lifted relation
{\mathcal{R}}^{*}
as going from
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}^{*}
to
\Pi_{j=1}^{m}B_{j}^{*}
, since
(\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})^{*}
is identified with
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}^{*}
(we are only working with uniform relations here).
Diagrammatically, this allows us to apply
\_^{*}
to individual wires:

{\mathcal{R}}^{*}
A_{1}^{*}
A_{n}^{*}
B_{1}^{*}
B_{m}^{*}
\vdots
\vdots
=
{\mathcal{R}}^{*}
(\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})^{*}
(\Pi_{j=1}^{m}B_{j})^{*}

When looking at uniform relations over words, one such regular
4
4

4

It is the behavior of the transducer
(\gamma_{A,A},A,A,A,I,F)
.
relation will be particularly useful, the
finite shift

\triangleright^{I\mid{}F}_{A}:A^{*}\to A^{*}
for a finite set
A
and two subsets
I,F\subseteq A
, defined as
\{(w,v)\mid\exists i\in I,\exists f\in F,iw=vf\}
. Said otherwise,
w
and
v
are related by the shift if either both are empty and
I\cap F\neq\varnothing
, or if neither are empty and the first letter of
v
is in
I
, the last letter of
w
is in
F
, and the remaining letters satisfy
w_{k}=v_{k+1}
. Diagrammatically, we represent the shift and its transposed:

I\mid F
I\mid F
A^{*}
A^{*}
A^{*}
A^{*}
\triangleright^{I\mid{}F}_{A}=
\left(\triangleright^{I\mid{}F}_{A}\right)^{t}=
,
.

The shift allow to obtain the behavior of a transducer in a compositional way.

Proposition 3.2

(Proved in
Section

B.2
)
.

For every transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,I,F)
, its behavior can be obtained by lifting
{\mathcal{T}}
and using the shift as follows:

{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
B^{*}
Q^{*}
I\mid F
A^{*}
Q^{*}
=
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,I,F)
A^{*}

This is however not the only way to represent transducers using relations. Another approach is to start from the category
FinRel
and freely add a feedback, state that a transducer
({\mathcal{T}},Q,B,Q,I,F)
should be able to be represented by the diagram on the right, and then find additional equations ensuring that this feedback operator behaves exactly as transducers do.

Formally, we define a graphical language
Trans
, so a syntactical construct, where the diagrams are seen as mathematical objects distinct from the relations they represent – which is a category where the objects are finite sets, and where the morphisms are generated by composing sequentially and in parallel the generators below together with the usual equations of a strict symmetric monoidal category (see
Figure

10
in the appendix for a reminder), the equations of a feedback category

, which is part of the completed equational theory.
(see

), and some equations ensuring we faithfully embed

(see

).

Equations for Faithfully Embedding

Let us point a couple of facts about this language:

•

A bundle of wires labeled
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
is the same as a single wire labeled
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
.

•

The wires and syntactical constructs are in
thick blue
, to distinguish them from actual relations.
{\mathcal{R}}
refers to an actual relation while
R
refers to an element of our language.

•

Double-line boxes denote the generator, while single-line boxes denote any diagram potentially constituted of many generators, including feedbacks.

•

The arrows on the feedback are a reminders that those are not the same as the cup and cap of
Section

2
.

•

Within
Figure

2
, the gray lines correspond to bracketing, and similarly to the equations of a strict symmetric monoidal category, the overall consequences of those equations is that bracketing can be safely ignored. Additionally, compared to the literature, those equations had to be adapted to the presence of labels on the feedback.

•

The equations of
Figure

3
ensures that if a diagram does not contain any instance of the “feedback” generator, we can merge all the generators into a single double-line box.

•

The current equational theory is incomplete, additional equations will be added in
Figure

4
to obtain completeness.

In order to ensure that the equations are not contradictory
6
6

6

Which would lead to the trivial category where all the diagrams are equal to one another.
, we provide a semantics and prove soundness of our equations. The semantics is a strong symmetric monoidal functor from
Trans
to
UniRel
, which we write
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\right\rrbracket}}
, and is actually simply “removing the color and adding a
\_^{*}
everywhere”. We provide an explicit definition in the appendix (see
Figure

13
). Soundness means that if one rewrites a diagram
R
into
S
using any of the listed equations, we still have
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{S}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
.
Completeness would be the other way around, whenever
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{S}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
then we could rewrite
R
into
S
(in other words,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\right\rrbracket}}
would be faithful).
While not having completeness yet, we still have a quasi-normal form, and universality for regular relations.

Proposition 3.3

(Quasi-Normal Form)
.

Any diagram of
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{R}}}}}\in\textbf{{Trans}}
from
A
to
B
can be put in the form on the right for some finite set
Q
and
{\mathcal{T}}\in\textbf{{FinRel}}
.

{\mathcal{T}}
B
Q
I\mid F
A
Q

Proof.

We start by using the first equation of
Figure

2
from right to left to push all the feedbacks at the bottom of the diagram. Then, we use the equations of
Figure

3
to merge all the non-feedback into a single box. Lastly, we use the last equation of
Figure

2
to merge all the feedbacks into a single feedback.
∎

Theorem 3.4

(Universality)
.

For all
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{S}}}}}\in\textbf{{Trans}}
,
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{S}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
is a regular relation. For all regular relation
{\mathcal{R}}
, there exists
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{S}}}}}\in\textbf{{Trans}}
such that
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{S}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}={\mathcal{R}}
.

Proof.

This follows from
Proposition

3.3
and
Proposition

3.2
.
∎

.

Figure 4:
Simulation Principle for Finite Words.

The core idea behind this principle is that a finite run can be see as a diagram starting with
I
, followed by a finite number of applications of
{\mathcal{R}}
and finally ends with
F
. In such diagram,
I
can turn into
J
by ”spawning” a
{\mathcal{S}}
, which can then move through
{\mathcal{R}}
, turning it into
{\mathcal{T}}
, and finally be absorbed by
F
to turn it into
G
, proving the equivalence with a run in the second transducer. This principle is sound with respect to
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\right\rrbracket}}
(this is proved formally in
Section

B.4
). The principle directly implies the sliding rule
Figure

5
allowing in particular to equate transducers with isomorphic sets of states. Notice that
Figure

5
allows for a
R
that might itself include some feedbacks.

{\mathcal{S}}(I)=\{d\in D\mid\exists c\in I,c\penalty\ {\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ d\}

{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(F)=\{c\in C\mid\exists d\in F,c\penalty\ {\mathcal{S}}\penalty\ d\}

Theorem 3.5

(Completeness)
.

For
R
and
T
two diagrams of
Trans
from
A
to
B
. Whenever
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{T}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
, we can rewrite
R
into
T
by using only the rules of
Trans
and
Figure

4
.

The core idea of the proof is diagrammatically mimic the determinization and minimization from automata theory. We recall that a finite automaton is simply a transducer with
{\mathds{1}}
for output alphabet, so we start by studying completeness in the case where
B={\mathds{1}}
.

Definition 3.2

(Determinization)
.

Let
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q,I,F)
be a finite automaton. We write
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{}
for the transitions within that automaton. Its determinization is the automata

({\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}}),A,{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q),\{I\},{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}_{\cap F\neq\varnothing}(Q))

More precisely, we start by recalling that
{\mathcal{P}}(Q)
is the set of all subsets of
Q
. We use
x,y
for elements of
Q
, and
X
,
Y
for elements of
{\mathcal{P}}(Q)
. Then, we define the function
7
7

7

We consider functions to be a special case of relations.

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}}):A\times{\mathcal{P}}(Q)\to{\mathcal{P}}(Q)
as

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})(a,X)=\{y\in Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ \exists x\in X,\penalty\ x\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a}y\}
.
We then consider the set
{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
of subsets of
Q
accessible by iteration of that function, starting from
I
. We can now restrict
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})
to a function
A\mapsto{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)\to{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
. Lastly,
{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}_{\cap F\neq\varnothing}(Q)
is the set of accessible subsets of
Q
that have a non-empty intersection with
F
.

Proposition 3.6
.

For all finite automata
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q,I,F)
, we have

\mathcal{P}({\mathcal{T}})
\ni
A
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
=
{\mathcal{T}}
\ni
Q
A
Q
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
\mathcal{P}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
Q

where
\ni:{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)\to Q
is the usual “contains” relation, that is
X\ni x
whenever
x\in X
.

Proof.

Using the logical reasoning as in
Section

2
, we can rewrite the equation as the following. We are looking at
\forall a\in A,\penalty\ \forall X\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q),\penalty\ \forall y\in Q,\penalty\

\left(\exists x\in Q,\penalty\ (x\in X)\land(x\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a}y)\right)\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \left(\exists Y\in{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q),\penalty\ (X\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})$}]{a}Y)\land(y\in Y)\right)

We start by reformulating the right side of the equivalence, as
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})
is a function, we can remove the
\exists
and write
y\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})(a,X)
. Then, using the definition of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})
, we obtain that it is equivalent to
\exists x\in X,x\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a}y
, which is exactly the left side of the equivalence.
∎

Rephrased in our graphical language and applyin the simulation principle, this gives:

Corollary 3.7
.

For any finite automaton
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q,I,F)
, using
Figure

, we have

A
Q
Q
I\mid F
=
A
{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
\{I\}\mid{\mathcal{P}}^{\textup{acc}}_{\cap F\neq\varnothing}(Q)
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})
{\mathcal{T}}

Another key element of our completeness result is the minimization of automata.

Definition 3.3

(Minimization)
.

Let
({\mathcal{D}},A,Q,\{i\},F)
be a
deterministic
finite automaton where every state is accessible from the initial state. We write
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{}
for the transitions within that automaton. We write
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w}
with
w
a word of size
n
for its iterated transition
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w_{n}}
. Its minimization is the deterministic automata

(L_{\mathcal{D}},A,L_{A^{*}},L_{\epsilon},L_{A^{*}}^{\ni\epsilon})
.

More precisely, we write
L_{w}=\{v\in A^{*}\mid\exists f\in F,i\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{wv}f\}
, and remark that whenever
L_{w}=L_{u}
, then for all
a\in A
we also have
L_{wa}=L_{ua}
. We then define
L_{A^{*}}=\{L_{w}\mid w\in A^{*}\}
note that a single element of this set might correspond to multiple distinct
w
, and in fact
L_{A^{*}}
is actually smaller or equal to
Q
in cardinality. We defined its restriction
L_{A^{*}}^{\ni\epsilon}=\{L_{w}\mid w\in A^{*},\epsilon\in L_{w}\}
. Lastly, we define the transition function as
L_{\mathcal{D}}(a,L_{w})=L_{wa}
.

This minimization is well known in the literature
[
HMU07
]
, and yields the unique smallest deterministic automaton equivalent to the starting one.

Proposition 3.8
.

For any deterministic finite automaton
({\mathcal{D}},A,Q,\{i\},F)
where every state is accessible from the initial state, we have

{\mathcal{D}}
{\mathcal{L}}
A
P
=
L_{\mathcal{D}}
{\mathcal{L}}
L_{A^{*}}
A
L_{A^{*}}
P
P
L_{A^{*}}

where
{\mathcal{L}}:Q\to L_{A^{*}}
relates
p\in Q
to
\ell\in L_{A^{*}}
whenever
\ell=\{v\mid\exists f\in F,p\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{v}f\}
. We note that since every state is accessible, there exists
w\in A^{*}
such that the later is equal to
L_{w}
.

The core idea is similar to
Proposition

3.6
: we express both sides of the equality as equations relating transitions of the transducers, and prove their equivalence using standard argument of automata minimization.
Again the simulation principle can be applied to conclude that the behavior of an automata and its minimization are the same.

Corollary 3.9
.

For any deterministic finite automaton
({\mathcal{D}},A,Q,\{i\},F)
where every state is accessible from the initial state, using

A

{\mathcal{D}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

For convenience, we name
({\mathcal{R}}_{M},B\times A,Q_{M},I_{M},F_{M})
the resulting minimal automaton. We do the same for
{\mathcal{S}}
and write
({\mathcal{S}}_{N},B\times A,Q_{N},I_{N},F_{N})
for the resulting minimal automaton. Since
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}={{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{S}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
and by soundness of the equations, we obtain that:

{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\hbox to111.21pt{\vbox to40.46pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 56.58362pt\lower-22.17464pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke=#000000} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill=#000000} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \lxSVG@closescope \lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} \hbox to0.0pt{
{

Using the definition of
{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\right\rrbracket}}
and
Proposition

3.2
, it follows that
({\mathcal{R}}_{M},B\times A,Q_{M},I_{M},F_{M})
and
({\mathcal{S}}_{N},B\times A,Q_{N},I_{N},F_{N})
recognize the same language, hence by uniqueness of the minimal automaton (see
[
HMU07
]
) we obtain that the two automata are equal up to an isomorphism
\iota:Q_{M}\to Q_{N}
, hence using the simulation principle with this
\iota
, we obtain

By combining with the cap
\eta_{B}
to bend the input
B
into an output, and using the the fact that
(\textbf{{id}}_{B}\times\epsilon_{B})\circ(\eta_{B}\times\textbf{{id}}_{B})=\textbf{{id}}_{B}
, we obtain
{{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{R}}}}}={{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0.5}\textbf{{S}}}}}
.
∎

The simulation principle requires to find a well chosen relation between the state spaces of two automata for the language equality to follow.
This is reminiscent of the well studied simulation techniques in automata theory, and it is not a coincidence.
In fact, the simulation principles also holds in the case where we only want language inclusion, simply by replacing every “=” of
Figure

4
by
\subseteq
(for a backward-simulation) or by
\supseteq
(for a forward-simulation). Thus, the simulation principle can be reinterpreted as asking to find a simulation which is at the same time forward and backward, the fact that finding a backward-forward simulations implies language equivalence has already appeared in the literature
[
LV95
]
.

4
Transducers on Bi-Infinite Words

We will now consider the case of transducers acting on bi-infinite words, i.e. words that are infinite both ways.
We call those transducers
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducers, and note that within them the notion of initial or final state becomes irrelevant, although one could argue that in fact all the states of those transducers are both initial and final.

4.1
{\mathbb{Z}}
-Transducers

Similarly to the
\_^{*}
lift, for
A
finite, we consider elements of
A^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
as infinite words
\dots w_{-1}w_{0}w_{1}\dots
, and we define the lifted relation
{\mathcal{R}}^{{\mathbb{Z}}}:A^{{\mathbb{Z}}}\to B^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
of
{\mathcal{R}}:A\to B
as
\{(w,v)\mid\forall i\in{\mathbb{Z}},(w_{i},v_{i})\in{\mathcal{R}}\}
.
Similarly to
UniRel
with
\_^{*}
, we write
{\mathbb{Z}}
-
Rel
for the compact closed strict symmetric monoidal category of relations over sets of the form
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and reuse the diagrammatic representations of
FinRel
in this setting. Similarly to
\_^{*}
, we can apply
\_^{{\mathbb{Z}}}
to individual wires as below, and this lifting operation is a faithful strong symmetric monoidal functor from
FinRel
to
{\mathbb{Z}}
-
Rel
.

{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
A_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}
A_{n}^{\mathbb{Z}}
B_{1}^{\mathbb{Z}}
B_{m}^{\mathbb{Z}}
\vdots
\vdots
=
{\mathcal{R}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
(\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i})^{\mathbb{Z}}
(\Pi_{j=1}^{m}B_{j})^{\mathbb{Z}}

We can also define
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducers – a.k.a transducers on bi-infinite words. They are simply tuples
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
similar to transducers but without a specified sets of initial or final states. When
B={\mathds{1}}
, such transducers corresponds to transition systems with finite set of states. When
A={\mathds{1}}
, they can also be interpreted as generalized symbolic dynamic systems. Indeed the set of elements of
Q
in relation with a symbol
b\in B
form an overlapping partition of
Q
, and the transition function can be interpreted as the action of a non-deterministic dynamic.

We reuse the same notations as for usual transducers, and note that a
run
within an
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer is now a bi-infinite sequence
\dots\xrightarrow[b_{-1}]{a_{-1}}q_{-1}\xrightarrow[b_{0}]{a_{0}}q_{0}\xrightarrow[b_{1}]{a_{1}}q_{1}\xrightarrow[b_{2}]{a_{2}}\dots
.
When such a run exists, we say that the transducer can transform the bi-infinite word
\dots a_{-1}a_{0}a_{1}\dots
into
\dots b_{-1}b_{0}\dots b_{1}\dots
.
The
behavior
of the transducer is the relation from
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
to
B^{\mathbb{Z}}
which relates
w
to
v
whenever the transducer can transform
w
into
v
. We write
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
for its behavior.
We define the
infinite shift

\triangleright_{A}:A^{\mathbb{Z}}\to A^{\mathbb{Z}}
as the relation
\{(w,v)\mid\forall k\in{\mathbb{Z}},w_{k}=v_{k+1}\}
.
Said otherwise,
\triangleright_{A}((a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}})=(a_{n-1})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
.
We often omit the alphabet
A
and only write
\triangleright
when the alphabet is clear from context and we represent it and its transposed:

A^{\mathbb{Z}}
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
\triangleright_{A}=
\left(\triangleright_{A}\right)^{t}=
,
. As in the finite case, we can obtain the behavior of a transducer from the shift.

Proposition 4.1
.

For every
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
, its behavior can be obtained using the lift of
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
and the shift as follows:

{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
B^{\mathbb{Z}}
Q^{\mathbb{Z}}
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
Q^{\mathbb{Z}}
=
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
B^{\mathbb{Z}}

In
Lemma

4.5
we will link the behavior of a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
to the behavior of the transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)
, that is, where all the states are initial and final.

4.2
Sofic Relations

We define the analog of regular languages and relations in the bi-infinite case.
We start with
sofic subshifts
which have been extensively studied in symbolic dynamics.

4.2.1
Sofic Subshifts, Factor-Closure and Limits

For
A
finite, we can define a distance on
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
as

\mathrm{d}(x,y)=2^{-\inf\{i\in{\mathbb{N}},x_{i}\neq y_{i}\vee x_{-i}\neq y_{-i}\}}
. The topology induced by this distance is called the prodiscrete topology (or Cantor topology), for which
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
is compact and closed. Subsets of
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
that are shift-invariant (meaning
\triangleright_{A}(X)=X
) and closed for the prodiscrete topology are called
subshifts
.

Definition 4.1

(Factor)
.

Given two potentially bi-infinite words
x,y\in A^{*}\cup A^{\mathbb{Z}}
, we say that
x
is a factor of
y
and write
x\sqsubseteq y
whenever there exists
u
,
v
such that
y=uxv
. Additionally, we say that is
x
is an internal factor of depth
n
, and write
x\sqsubseteq_{n}y
, whenever both
u
and
v
are of length at least
n
(including infinity). For
x\in A^{\mathbb{Z}}
, we then define
\mathrm{Fact}(x):=\{u\in A^{*}|u\sqsubseteq x\}
. Similarly, for
X
a subshift or a language, we define
\mathrm{Fact}(X):=\{u\in A^{*}|\exists x\in X,u\sqsubseteq x\}
. A language
L
is said
factor-closed
if
\mathrm{Fact}(L)=L
.

A subshift
X\subseteq A^{\mathbb{Z}}
is said to be
sofic
8
8

8

There are several equivalent definitions of sofic subshifts, we briefly discuss this in Appendix
C.1
if
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
is regular.
More than that, a sofic subshift can actually be generated by its factors with a well-chosen notion of limit.

For the remaining of this section, whenever we consider words of
A^{*}
, we consider them to be indexed as if they were words of
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
.
By default,
u\in A^{*}
is indexed from
0
to
|u|-1
but we write
\triangleright^{k}(u)
for its
k
-shifted version indexed from
-k
to
-k+|u|-1
. Whenever
i
is not a valid index for
u
, we write
u_{i}=\bot
.
This allows us to extend
9
9

9

The formula is unchanged, that is
\mathrm{d}(x,y)=2^{-\inf\{i\in{\mathbb{N}},x_{i}\neq y_{i}\vee x_{-i}\neq y_{-i}\}}
; see Appendix
C.1
.
the distance
\mathrm{d}(u,x)
to a finite word
u
and an infinite word
x
.

Definition 4.2

(Limit Language)
.

Given a language
L\subseteq A^{*}
, we define its limit language
\overrightarrow{L}
as the set of bi-infinite words that can be reached as a limit of words in
L
, that is :

\overrightarrow{L}:=\{x\in A^{\mathbb{Z}},\exists(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in L^{\mathbb{N}},(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}\in\mathbb{N}^{\mathbb{N}}\ s.t.\ \lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})=x\}

Lemma 4.2
.

Given a language
L\subseteq A^{*}
, its limit language
\overrightarrow{L}
is a subshift.

It is clear that different languages can have the same limit, typically a language and its factor closure have the same limit,
however there is a canonical language for a given sofic subshift.
The idea is to remove all the words that are not relevant for the limit behavior.

Definition 4.3

(Pruned Language)
.

Given a language
L\subseteq A^{*}
, we define its pruned sub-language
\mathrm{Prun}(L)
as the set of words in
L
that have arbitrarily long (simultaneous) left and right extensions that is :
\mathrm{Prun}(L):=\{u\in L,\forall n\in\mathbb{N},\exists v\in L,u\sqsubseteq_{n}v\}
.
A language
L
is said to be
pruned
when
L=\mathrm{Prun}(L)
, or equivalently whenever for every
w\in L
there exists two non-empty words
u,v
such that
uwv\in L
.

Proposition 4.3
.

\mathrm{Fact}(-):{\mathcal{P}}(A^{\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathcal{P}}(A^{*})
can be restricted to a bijection between sofic subshifts and pruned factor-closed regular languages, and has
\overrightarrow{-}:{\mathcal{P}}(A^{*})\to{\mathcal{P}}(A^{\mathbb{Z}})
for inverse when restricted similarly.

This result allows to reduce questions on sofic subshifts to questions on pruned factor-closed regular languages.

4.2.2
Presentation of a Sofic Subshift

We have established a bijection between sofic subshifts and factor closed-regular languages, now building on this we introduce a normal form for automata that allows us to reproduce our completeness result to the infinite case.

Definition 4.4

(Presentation of a Sofic Subshift)
.

A
presentation

({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
of a sofic subshift
X
is an automaton
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q,Q,Q)
, meaning all the states are initial and final, recognizing a language
L
and such that
\overrightarrow{L}=X
. A presentation is said

Pruned

if its language is pruned, meaning that for every word
w
in the language, there exists
u
and
v
both non-empty such that
uwv
is in the language.

Right-Resolving

whenever its transition relation is a partial function.

Rooted

whenever there exists a root state
r
such that

•

every accepted word admits an accepted run starting by
r
,

•

and every state is accessible from
r
, meaning there is a run from
r
to that state.

Theorem 4.4

(Uniqueness)
.

Every non-empty
10
10

10

If the sofic subshift is empty, its only pruned presentation is the empty automaton, but this automaton is not rooted.
sofic subshift admits a unique minimal rooted right-resolving pruned presentation, up to isomorphism.

Notice that this result is not in contradiction with the well known fact that the minimal presentation of a sofic subshift is not unique in general
[
LM21
]
. Indeed, it is often possible to find non-rooted presentations that are smaller than our “minimal” one, but it is from this rootedness that we obtain uniqueness.

4.2.3
Sofic Relations and
{\mathbb{Z}}
-Transducers

A relation
{\mathcal{R}}:A^{\mathbb{Z}}\to B^{\mathbb{Z}}
is said sofic if it is a sofic subshift when seen as a subset of
(A\times B)^{\mathbb{Z}}
, which we know is equivalent to having a representation
({\mathcal{T}},A\times B,Q)
. Using the usual equivalence between automaton on
A\times B
and transducers from
A
to
B
, and a relation
{\mathcal{R}}:A^{\mathbb{Z}}\to B^{\mathbb{Z}}
is sofic if and only if there exists a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
such that
\overrightarrow{{\mathcal{L}}(T,A,B,Q,Q,Q)}={\mathcal{R}}
. Using the following lemma,
{\mathcal{R}}
is sofic if and only if it is the behavior of a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer.

Lemma 4.5
.

Given a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducers
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
, we consider the transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)
where all the states are initial and final.
We have
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)=\overrightarrow{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)}
.

The composition or product of two sofic relations is sofic, one can simply build the associated
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer using the exact same construction as in
Proposition

3.1
, so we write
SofRel
for the subcategory of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-
Rel
of sofic relations.

4.3
Diagrams for Subshifts and Completeness

I\mid F
\mapsto

The graphical language
{\mathbb{Z}}
-Trans
is a variant of
Trans
.
Its definition and equational theory (without simulation principle) are exactly the same, up to changing
thick blue
into
thick red
and applying the substitution on the right

We refer to
Section

D.2
for an explicit definition.
Adapting the simulation principle is more complex, so we will treat it explicitly in the core of this paper.
We write
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\right\rrbracket}}
for its semantics, going from
{\mathbb{Z}}
-Trans
to
{\mathbb{Z}}
-Rel
, which is simply “removing the color and adding a
\_^{\mathbb{Z}}
everywhere”. The explicit definition of this semantics is given in
Figure

22
.
As in the finite case, even without a simulation principle, we have a quasi-normal form and our language is universal for sofic relations, see
Sections

D.4
and
D.5
for the exact statement.

Adapting the simulation principle to this infinite case is non-trivial, as we cannot simply remove the preconditions that acted on
I
and
F
and expect the equation to be sound. We propose in
Figure

6
some relatively complex preconditions.

Figure 6:
Simulation Principle for Bi-Infinite Words, and an Equation Deducible from it.

Informally, the second precondition means that any state of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,D)
that is the start of a path of size
\textup{card}(D)
(hence a loop must exists, hence it is the start of an infinite path) must be in the domain of
{\mathcal{S}}
. Conversely, the third precondition means that any state of the
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer
({\mathcal{R}},A,B,C)
that is the end of a path of size
\textup{card}(C)
(hence a loop must exists, hence it is the end of an infinite path) must be in the codomain of
{\mathcal{S}}
. We note that most of the time this equation is used in the case where
{\mathcal{S}}
is a total and surjective relation (that is
{\mathcal{S}}^{-1}(C)=D
and
{\mathcal{S}}(D)=C
), which automatically satisfies the second and third preconditions.
Like in the finite case, we can derive from the simulation principle one of the well-known equations of trace monoidal categories.
The only missing equation to obtain a trace monoidal category is that adding a feedback loop to
\gamma_{A,A}
yields the identity
\textbf{{id}}_{A}
(which would require to remove the “shift” from our feedback).
We can now state the completeness theorem.

Theorem 4.6

(Completeness)
.

For
R
and
T
two diagrams of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-Trans
from
A
to
B
. If
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}={{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{T}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
, we can rewrite
R
into
T
by using only the rules of
{\mathbb{Z}}
-Trans
and
Figure

6
.

While the proof still relies on the uniqueness of minimal deterministic automatons, two differences are notable: (1) Our “automaton” is now a presentation of a sofic subshift, meaning that we rely on
Theorem

4.4
instead of the usual theorem about uniqueness of the minimal automaton. (2) Before the determinization step, we need to prune all the states of the presentation that are not part of any bi-infinite path.

5
Conclusion

The diagrammatical axiomatization presented in this paper presents many similarities with recent works also relying on feedback loops to represent internal state space.
There was a thread of work aiming to handle diagrammatically streams of data, first
[
SK19
]
in the classical case and then
[
DFR22
]
and
[
CDP21
]
in the probabilistic and quantum cases.
Our simulation principle bears similarity with equations appearing in
[
GKS22
]
.
In
[
PZ+21
]
, the authors provide a complete diagrammatical theory for finite automata with similar goals, however the structure or the categories involved is very different as they do not use the cartesian product as monoidal structure.
However, we do not know of any attempt to handle subshifts with such formalism.
Unifying transducers and sofic subshifts with similar graphical languages opens many research directions towards diagrammatical proofs in symbolic dynamics. We present a few of them.

Subshifts on
\mathbb{N}
A straightforward next step would be to consider the situation in-between finite and bi-infinite words. Indeed, this would allow to draw interesting bridges between symbolic dynamics on the half-line, Buchi automata and LTS with initial states. This direction would also allow easier comparison with the monoidal stream line of research.

One-dimensional SFTs
One of the most natural objects of symbolic dynamics are subshift of finite type (SFTs), defined by a finite number of local constraints.
Surprisingly, their diagrammatical definition is less straightforward than sofic ones.
The logical next step would be to define SFTs diagrammatically and use this language to show classical results linking sofic subshifts, SFTs and cellular automata.

Symbolic dynamics on groups
Symbolic dynamics also studies infinite colorings of
{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}
and more generally Cayley graphs of finitely generated groups.
Diagrammatical proofs seem promising to tackle this since they seem to abstract the topology of the graph, ”hidden” in the shift relation.
This would suggest that some diagrammatical one-dimensional proof techniques may be adapted to higher dimensions and groups quite straightforwardly.

General induction principle
Many objects in symbolic dynamics have a coinductive definition (substitutive subshifts, limit sets of cellular automata, …).
Generalizing our simulation principle to more abstract categories could provide new proofs techniques usable for all these co-inductive objects in an abstract way.

Finally, abstracting our diagrams to other settings/categories than relations may also allow us to study other problems such as counting subshifts, studying ergodic probability measures, and defining a notion of quantum subshifts.

Equations for the Cup and Cap

All the diagrams of
Section

2
can actually be formalized using string diagrams from category theory
[
Mac98
]
. Indeed, finite sets and relations is well known to be a strict symmetric monoidal category, called
FinRel
, that is:

•

A set of objects (finite sets).

•

A binary operation on objects (the Cartesian product
\times
) which is associative and has a neutral element (
\mathds{1}
).

•

A set of morphisms between those objects (relations), a way to compose them (the usual composition
\circ
) that is associative and has a neutral element (the identity
id
).

•

A binary operations on morphisms (the Cartesian product of relations
\times
) which is associative, has a neutral element (
\textbf{{id}}_{\mathds{1}}
), and is a bifunctor (see the last equation of
Figure

7
).

•

For every two objects
A
and
B
, a morphism that swaps them (
\gamma_{A,B}
) which forms a natural isomorphism (third and fourth equations of
Figure

7
).

We list all the resulting equations in
Figure

7
, both in text and diagrams. In those diagrams, gray boxes correspond to “bracketing”, and the overall consequence of those equations is that we can keep the bracketing implicit.

If we account for the cup and cap, relations form a compact closed category (where additionally all the objects are self-duals)
[
KL80
]
, which correspond to the additional equations listed in
Figure

8
.

Appendix B
Proofs for Finite Words

B.1
Composing Transducers

In this section, we prove
Proposition

3.1
by showing that we can compose and make a product of transducers.

Given two transducers
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,I,F)
and
({\mathcal{S}},C,D,P,J,G)
, we can build their product as
({\mathcal{R}},A\times C,B\times D,Q\times P,I\times J,F\times G)
where
((a,c),(q,p))\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ ((b,d),(q^{\prime},p^{\prime}))
whenever
(a,q)\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ (b,q^{\prime})
and
(c,p)\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ (d,p^{\prime})
. Its behavior is exactly the product of the two behaviors.
Assuming
B=C
, we can build their composition as
({\mathcal{U}},A,D,Q\times P,I\times J,F\times G)
where
(a,(q,p))\penalty\ {\mathcal{U}}\penalty\ (d,(q^{\prime},p^{\prime}))
whenever there exists
b\in B
such that
(a,q)\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ (b,q^{\prime})
and
(b,p)\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ (d,p^{\prime})
. Its behavior is exactly the composition of the two behaviors.

B.2
From Transducers to Graphical Representation

In this section, we prove
Proposition

3.2
, that is for every transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,I,F)
, its behavior can be obtained by lifting
{\mathcal{T}}
and using the shift as follows:

{\mathcal{T}}^{*}
B^{*}
Q^{*}
I\mid F
A^{*}
Q^{*}
=
{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,I,F)
A^{*}
B^{*}

We write
{\mathcal{R}}
for the left-hand-side. We take
w\in A^{*}
and
v\in B^{*}
of size
n
, and note that by definition of the composition of relations,
w\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ v
if and only there exists two words
u,t\in Q^{*}
such that
(w,u)\penalty\ {\mathcal{T}}^{*}\penalty\ (v,t)
and
u\penalty\ \left(\triangleright^{I\mid{}F}\right)^{t}\penalty\ t
.

Let us focus on the former, it is equivalent to, for every
1\leq k\leq n
,
(w_{k},u_{k})\penalty\ {\mathcal{T}}\penalty\ (v_{k},t_{k})
, that is
u_{k}\xrightarrow[v_{k}]{w_{k}}t_{k}
.

Let us focus on the latter, it is equivalent to the first letter of
u
being in
I
, the last letter of
t
being in
F
, and for all
1\leq k\leq n
,
u_{k+1}=t_{k}
. Said otherwise, there exists a sequence
q_{0},q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\in Q
such that
u=q_{0}\dots q_{n-1}
,
t=q_{1}\dots q_{n}
,
q_{0}\in I
and
q_{n}\in F
.

Coming back to the initial equivalence, we have
w\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ v
if and only if there exists a sequence
q_{0},q_{1},\dots,q_{n}\in Q
such that
q_{0}\in I
,
q_{n}\in F
, and for all
1\leq k\leq n
,
q_{k-1}\xrightarrow[v_{k}]{w_{k}}q_{k}
. Since this is exactly the definition of a run, we have that
w\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ v
if and only if
w\penalty\ {\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,I,F)\penalty\ v
.

B.5
Minimization of finite transducers

We provide the full proof of
Proposition

3.8
.

Using the logical reasoning as in
Section

2
, we can rewrite the equation as the following. We are looking at
\forall a\in A,\forall p\in Q,\forall\ell\in L_{A^{*}},

\begin{array}[]{c}\left(\exists q\in Q,\penalty\ (p\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{a}q)\land(\ell=\{w\mid\exists f\in F,q\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w}f\})\right)\\
\Leftrightarrow\\
\left(\exists m\in L_{A^{*}},\penalty\ (m=\{v\mid\exists f\in F,p\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{v}f\})\land(m\xrightarrow[\text{\small$L_{\mathcal{D}}$}]{a}\ell)\right)\end{array}

Before studying either side of that equivalence, we note that since
p
is accessible from the initial state, there exists a word
u\in A^{*}
such that
i\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{u}p
, hence
L_{u}=\{v\mid\exists f\in F,p\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{v}f\}
.

We start by reformulating the second part of the equivalence. Since the formula for
m
is given, we can remove the
\exists m
and obtain
L_{u}\xrightarrow[\text{\small$L_{\mathcal{D}}$}]{a}\ell
. We can now use the definition of
L_{\mathcal{D}}
and simplify the later in
\ell=L_{ua}
.

We now look at the first part of the equivalence, since
{\mathcal{D}}
is deterministic,
q
is uniquely determined so we can remove the
\exists q
and we obtain
\ell=\{w\mid\exists f\in F,{\mathcal{D}}(a,p)\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w}f\}
. Since
i\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{u}p
, this is equivalent to
\ell=\{w\mid\exists f\in F,i\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{uaw}f\}
, that is
\ell=L_{ua}
.

∎

Appendix C
Proofs for the Sofic Subshifts

C.1
Sofic subshifts and pruned factor-closed language

Here we present the proofs that where omitted in
Section

4.2
and a few additional minor or intermediate results on the correspondence between sofic subshifts and pruned factor-closed languages.

First recall that, in Section
4.2
, we define sofic subshifts as the subshifts whose factor language is regular.
This may differ from the definition with which the reader is familiar with, as a more usual definition is that a susbshift is sofic when it can be defined as the image of a SFT (subshift of finite type) by a cellular automaton.

Note that we deal here only with symbolic dynamics in dimension 1, that is the full shift is
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
for some finite alphabet
A
.
Hence another definition of a sofic shift is a subshift
X
which can be defined by a regular language
\mathcal{F}
of forbidden words. Denote
A^{*}\cdot\mathcal{F}\cdot A^{*}
the set of finite word containing a word in
\mathcal{F}
as a factor subword.
Now remark that the factor language of
X
is precisely the complement of
A^{*}\cdot\mathcal{F}\cdot A^{*}
, since
\mathcal{F}
is regular then
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
is also regular.
Conversely if
X
is a subshift with regular factor language
{\mathcal{L}}
, then it can be defined as avoiding the complement of
{\mathcal{L}}
which is also a regular language.

For the same considerations (though with a different terminology) one may refer to
[
LM21
]
.

We can refine the factor relation
x\sqsubseteq y
into
x\subset y
by asking for the indexes to match. More precisely, for
u
a finite word and
x
a bi-infinite word, we write
\triangleright^{k}(u)\subset x
whenever
u=x_{-k}\dots x_{-k+|u|-1}
. We remark that
\mathrm{d}(x,\triangleright^{k}(u))=\sup\{\mathrm{d}(x,y)|y\in A^{\mathbb{Z}}\ s.t.\ \triangleright^{k}(u)\subset y\}
.

Remark C.1

(Comparing finite and infinite words)
.

For simplicity we extend the distance
\mathrm{d}
to compare finite words and infinite words.
However this does not define a metric on the mixed space
A^{*}\cup A^{\mathbb{Z}}
.

The most rigorous approach to talk of a convergence of a sequence of finite words to an infinite word is that of cylinders.

The
cylinder
of word
u
at position
k
, denoted by
[u]_{k}
, is the set of all bi-infinite words that coincide with
u
at position
k
that is

[u]_{k}:=\{x\in A^{\mathbb{Z}}|\forall 0\leq i<|u|,x_{i-k}=u_{i}\}

In our notation this corresponds to
[u]_{k}=\{x\in A^{\mathbb{Z}}|\triangleright^{k}(u)\subset x\}
.

We call
domain
of a cylinder
[u]_{k}
the integer interval
\llbracket-k,|u|-k-1\rrbracket
, and we denote it here by
\mathcal{D}([u]_{k})
.

Given a family
(C_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of cylinders, if
\mathcal{D}(C_{n})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{Z}}
, then there exists a sub-family
(C_{f(n)})_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
, for each
n
a larger cylinder
C_{f(n)}\subset C_{n}^{\prime}
and an infinite word
x
such that
\{x\}=\bigcap\limits_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}C_{n}^{\prime}
.

In particular if
\mathcal{D}(C_{n})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}{\mathbb{Z}}
and for all
n
,
C_{n+1}\subseteq C_{n}
then
\bigcap\limits_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}C_{n}=\{x\}
for some bi-inifinite word
x
.

The easiest case is when each cylinder is some
C_{n}=[u_{n}]_{k_{n}}
such that
u_{n}\sqsubseteq_{1}u_{n+1}
with
u_{n+1}=v\cdot u_{n}\cdot v^{\prime}
,
|v|=k_{n+1}-k_{n}>0
and
|v^{\prime}|>0
.
In this case we indeed have
\bigcap\limits_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}C_{n}=\{x\}
which, outside this remark, we write
\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}x
.

Remark C.2

(Intersection of cylinders compared to limit of words)
.

The notion of limit of words is a bit more permissive than that of intersection of cylinders.
Take for example the sequence of word
u_{n}:=1\cdot 0^{2n+1}\cdot 1
and positions
k_{n}=n+1
.
We have
\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}0^{\mathbb{Z}}
, but
\bigcup\limits_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}[u_{n}]_{k_{n}}=\emptyset
.
However, taking
v_{n}=0^{2n+1}
and
k_{n}^{\prime}=n
, we have
[u_{n}]_{k_{n}}\subset[v_{n}]_{k_{n}^{\prime}}
as
u_{n}=1\cdot v_{n}\cdot 1
and
\bigcap\limits_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}[v_{n}]_{k_{n}^{\prime}}=\{0^{\mathbb{Z}}\}
.

We now prove Lemma
4.2
which states that any limit language
\overrightarrow{L}
is a subshift.

Proof of Lemma
4.2
.

Let
L\subseteq A^{*}
.
First remark that
\overrightarrow{L}
is straightforwardly shift invariant, because if
x\in\overrightarrow{L}
then by definition
x
is some limit of
\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})
and simply shifting the sequence of indices by
k
proves that
\triangleright^{k}(x)
is in
\overrightarrow{L}
as
\triangleright^{k+k_{n}}(u_{n})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}\triangleright^{k}(x)
.

Now we prove similarly that
\overrightarrow{L}
is closed. Let
y\in A^{\mathbb{Z}}
be the limit of a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of infinite words in
\overrightarrow{L}
.
First let us remark that the sequence of central factors of
x_{n}
also converges to
y
, that is
(x_{n})_{-n}\dots(x_{n})_{n}\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}y
.
Since
x_{n}\in\overrightarrow{L}
, it is the limit of some sequence
(\triangleright^{k_{n,m}}{u_{n,m}})_{m\in\mathbb{N}}
where the
k_{n,m}
are integers and
u_{n,m}
words in
L
. Hence for each
n
there exists
f(n)
such that
\triangleright^{k_{n,f(n)}}(u_{n,f(n)})
contains
(x_{n})_{-n}\dots(x_{n})_{n}
and we now have that
\triangleright^{k_{n,f(n)}}(u_{n,f(n)})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}y
. Hence
y
is in
\overrightarrow{L}
and so
\overrightarrow{L}
is closed.
∎

Lemma C.3
.

A regular language is factor-closed if and only if it is recognized by a finite automaton where all states are initial and final. Furthermore, all the states of the minimal automaton of a factor-closed language are final.

Proof.

First given an automata
\mathcal{A}
where all states are initial and final and a word
u
recognized by
\mathcal{A}
, from an accepting run of
u
in
\mathcal{A}
we can directly read accepting runs for all factors of
u
, so the language recognized by
\mathcal{A}
is factor-closed.

Now, we consider a factor closed regular language
L
.
There exist a minimal deterministic automaton
\mathcal{A}
recognizing
L
.
As
L
is factor closed and
\mathcal{A}
is determistic, every accessible and co-accessible state (
i.e.
, on a path from an initial state to an accepting state) is accepting.
By minimality, all states are accessible and co-accessible so all states are accepting.
Now considering
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
where all states are also initial states.

\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
also recognizes
L
. First we have that
L
is included in the language recognized by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
because any accepting run in
\mathcal{A}
is also accepting in
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
.
Now we prove that the recognized language is exactly
L
.
Let
u
be a word accepted by
\mathcal{A}^{\prime}
, it is accepted by a path starting on some state
q
, as
q
is accessible in
\mathcal{A}
there exist a word
v
such that
v\cdot u
is accepted on a path starting from an initial state
q_{0}
of
\mathcal{A}
. Therefore
v\cdot u
is in
L
, by factor closure
u
is also in
L
.
∎

Lemma C.4

(Limit and pruning)
.

Given a language
L\subseteq A^{*}
, we have
\mathrm{Fact}(\overrightarrow{L})=\mathrm{Fact}(\mathrm{Prun}(L))
.

This result is quite natural as factors of limit words are precisely the words that have arbitrarily long left-and-right extensions in the language.

Proof.

We prove this lemma by double inclusion. Let
L
be a language.
First we prove
\mathrm{Fact}(\overrightarrow{L})\subseteq\mathrm{Fact}(\mathrm{Prun}(L))
.
Let
v\in\mathrm{Fact}(\overrightarrow{L})
, that is there exists
x\in\overrightarrow{L}
such that
v\sqsubseteq x
, that is, there exists
k\in\mathbb{Z}

\triangleright^{k}(v)\subset x
.
By definition of
\overrightarrow{L}
, there exists two sequences
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of words in
L
and
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of integers such that
\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})=x
.
Denote
k^{\prime}=|k|+|v|
, by definition for any integer
l
and for any word
v^{\prime}
such that
d(x,v^{\prime})\leq 2^{-k^{\prime}-l}
we have
x_{[-k^{\prime}-l,k^{\prime}+l]}\sqsubseteq v^{\prime}
and since
v\sqsubseteq x_{[-k^{\prime},k^{\prime}]}
we have
v\sqsubseteq v^{\prime}
and the existence of
v_{p},v_{s}
of length at least
l
such that
v_{p}vv_{s}=v^{\prime}
.
In particular as
(\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n}))
tends to
x
, there exists
n_{0}
such that

d(\triangleright^{k_{n_{0}}}(u_{n_{0}}),x)\sqsubseteq 2^{-k^{\prime}}
so that
v\sqsubseteq u_{n_{0}}\in L
.
Now repeat the same reasoning with
u_{n_{0}}
, and denote
k^{\prime\prime}=|k_{n_{0}}|+|u_{n_{0}}|
.
As
(\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n}))
tends to
x
, for any
l
there exists
n
such that we have
d(\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n}),x)\sqsubseteq 2^{-k^{\prime\prime}-l}
so that
u_{n_{0}}\sqsubseteq u_{n}
with a prefix
u_{p}
and a suffix
u_{s}
of length at least
l
such that
u_{p}u_{n_{0}}u_{s}=u_{n}
.
That is
v\sqsubseteq u_{n_{0}}
and
u_{n_{0}}\in\mathrm{Prun}(L)
so that
v\in\mathrm{Fact}(\mathrm{Prun}(L))
.

Now the second inclusion.
Let
u\in\mathrm{Fact}(\mathrm{Prun}(L))
, that is
u\sqsubseteq u^{\prime}\in\mathrm{Prun}(L)
.
For simplicity assume that
|u^{\prime}|
is odd and denote
k^{\prime}=\frac{|u^{\prime}|-1}{2}
.
That is, for any
n
there exists
u_{p,n},u_{s,n}\in A^{\geq n}
such that
u_{p,n}u^{\prime}u_{s,n}\in L
.
Denote
k_{n}=|u_{p,n}|+k^{\prime}
and
u_{n}=u_{p,n}u^{\prime}u_{s,n}
.
By compactness of
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
(\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n}))
admits a converging subsequence. Denote
x
its limit, which by definition is in
\overrightarrow{L}
.
Since
u^{\prime}
is at the center of each
\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})
, more precisely
\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})|_{[-k^{\prime},k^{\prime}]}=u^{\prime}
, so we have
u^{\prime}\sqsubseteq x
and more precisely
x|_{[-k^{\prime},k^{\prime}]}=u^{\prime}
. So
u\sqsubseteq u^{\prime}\sqsubseteq x
and
u\in\mathrm{Fact}(\overrightarrow{L})
.
∎

Definition C.1

(Orbit, closure)
.

Given a set
X\subseteq A^{\mathbb{Z}}
we define its orbit
\mathcal{O}(X)
as the set of all possible shifts of words in
X
, that is
\mathcal{O}(X):=\{\triangleright^{k}(x),k\in\mathbb{Z},x\in X\}
.

We also define its orbit-closure
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{O}(X))
as the topological closure of its orbit.

As subshifts are precisely sets of bi-inifinte words that are shift-invariant and closed, a set
X\subseteq A^{\mathbb{Z}}
is a subshifts if and only if
X=\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{O}(X))
. And more generaly
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{O}(X))
is the smallest subshift containing
X
.

Lemma C.5
.

For any set
X
of infinite words we have
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{Fact}(X)}=\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{O}(X))
.

Proof.

Let
y\in\mathrm{cl}(X)
, by definition there exists a sequence
(x_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of elements of
\mathcal{O}(X)
such that
x_{n}\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}y
, by definition there exists for each
n
,
x_{n}^{\prime}\in X
and
k_{n}\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
x_{n}=\triangleright^{k_{n}}(x_{n}^{\prime})
.
Denote
\triangleright^{n}(u_{n}):=x_{n}|_{[-n,n]}
, that is the central word on length
2n+1
in
x_{n}
.
We have for each
n
,
u_{n}
in
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
as
u_{n}\sqsubseteq x_{n}=\triangleright^{k_{n}}(x_{n}^{\prime})
, and we have by definition that
\triangleright^{n}(u_{n})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}y
so that
y\in\overrightarrow{\mathrm{Fact}(X)}
.

Conversely, let
y\in\overrightarrow{\mathrm{Fact}(X)}
. There exists a sequence
(u_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of words in
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
and a sequence
(k_{n})_{n\in\mathbb{N}}
of offsets such that
\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})\underset{n\to\infty}{\longrightarrow}y
.
By definition for each
u_{n}
there exists a
x_{n}
in
\mathcal{O}(X)
such that
\triangleright^{-|u_{n}|/2}(u_{n})\subset x_{n}
.
By definition of the distance on
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
the sequence
x_{n}
also converges and to the same limit
y
. Hence
y
is in
\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{O}(X))
.
∎

Lemma C.6
.

For any set
X\subseteq A^{\mathbb{Z}}
,
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
is pruned and factor-closed.

Proof.

Let
X\subseteq A^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
L=\mathrm{Fact}(X)
.

L
is factor closed, indeed for any
u\in L
, there exists
x\in X
such that
u\sqsubseteq x
by definition of
\mathrm{Fact}(-)
. For any
v\in L
we have
v\sqsubseteq u\sqsubseteq x
so
v\in\mathrm{Fact}(x)\subset\mathrm{Fact}(X)=L
.
So we have
L=\mathrm{Fact}(L)
.

L
is pruned, indeed for any
u\in L
, there exists
x\in X
and
k\in\mathbb{Z}
such that
\triangleright^{k}(u)\subset x
.
Denote
k_{n}=k+n
and
u_{n}=x_{-k-n}\dots x_{-k+|u|+n}
.
For each
n
we have
u_{n}\in\mathrm{Fact}(x)\subseteq L
and we have
u\sqsubseteq_{n}u_{n}
. Hence
u\in\mathrm{Prun}(L)
and
L
is pruned.
∎

We now prove Proposition
4.3
which states that
\mathrm{Fact}():{\mathcal{P}}(A^{\mathbb{Z}})\to{\mathcal{P}}(A^{*})
is, when restricted from sofic subshifts to pruned factor-closed regular languages, is a bijection.

Proof of Proposition
4.3
.

In this proof we denote
\mathrm{Sofic}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}(A^{\mathbb{Z}})
the set of sofic subshifts,
\mathrm{Reg}\subseteq{\mathcal{P}}(A^{*})
the set of regular languages and
\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{fc}}_{\mathrm{p}}\subseteq\mathrm{Reg}
the set of factor-closed and pruned regular languages.

Let
L\in\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{fc}}_{\mathrm{p}}
, then by
Lemma

C.4
,
\mathrm{Fact}(\overrightarrow{L})=\mathrm{Fact}(\mathrm{Prun}(L))=L
.

Let
X\in\mathrm{Sofic}
, by
Lemma

C.5
,
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{Fact}(X)}=\mathrm{cl}(\mathcal{O}(X))=X
and since
X
is sofic,
\mathrm{Fact}(\overrightarrow{\mathrm{Fact}(X)})=\mathrm{Fact}(X)
is regular. It is also pruned and factor-closed by
Lemma

C.6
.

Therefore the restriction
\mathrm{Fact}(-):\mathrm{Sofic}\to\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{fc}}_{\mathrm{p}}
is bijective and its inverse is the restriction
\overrightarrow{-}:\mathrm{Reg}^{\mathrm{fc}}_{\mathrm{p}}\to\mathrm{Sofic}
.
∎

C.2
Characterization of the Behavior of a Transducer

We prove Lemma
4.5
which states that the behavior of the infinite transducer is the limit of the behavior of the corresponding finite transducer, that is
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)=\overrightarrow{{\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)}
.

Proof of Lemma
4.5
.

Let
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
be a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer.

Denote
X_{{\mathcal{T}}}\subset(A\times B)^{\mathbb{Z}}
its behavior, that is
X_{{\mathcal{T}}}={\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
.
And
{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}\subset(A\times B)^{*}
the behavior of the finite transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)
, that is
{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}={\mathcal{L}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)
.

We prove
X_{{\mathcal{T}}}=\overrightarrow{{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}}
.

This holds because an infinite run in
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
is precisely the limit of an increasing sequence of finite runs in
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)
.

We prove that
\overrightarrow{{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}}\subseteq X_{{\mathcal{T}}}
.
First recall that since all states are initial and final,
{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
is pruned and factor-closed. Hence any sequence
(\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n}))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
of words in
{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
that converge to a bi-infinite word
x
can be taken as strictly increasing that is
\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})\sqsubseteq_{1}\triangleright^{k_{n+1}}(u_{n+1})
.
Take such a sequence
(\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n}))
in
{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
.
For each
n
,
u_{n}=(a_{n,0},b_{n,0})\dots(a_{n,k},b_{n,k})
admits a run in
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)
denote
u_{n}^{\prime}
the word in
(A\times B\times Q)
such that for each
i
we have
q_{n,i}\xrightarrow[a_{n,i}]{b_{n}{i}}q_{n,i+1}
.

As
(A\times B\times Q)^{\mathbb{Z}}
is compact, the sequence
(\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n}^{\prime}))_{n\in{\mathbb{N}}}
has subsequence that converges to some bi-infinite word
y
.
By hypothesis the projection of
y
on the first two components of each letter is
x
and
y
is the trace of an infinite run in
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
and hence
x\in X_{{\mathcal{T}}}
.

Now we prove the converse, that is
X_{{\mathcal{T}}}\subseteq\overrightarrow{{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}}
.
Let
x=((a_{i},b_{i}))_{i\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\in X_{{\mathcal{T}}}
, there exists a sequence of states
(q_{i})_{i\in{\mathbb{N}}}
such that

…\xrightarrow[a_{-1}]{b_{-1}}q_{0}\xrightarrow[a_{0}]{b_{0}}q_{1}\xrightarrow[a_{1}]{b_{1}}
is a bi-infinite run in
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
.
For each
n
, let
k_{n}=n
and
u_{n}=(a_{-n},b_{-n})\dots(a_{n},b_{n})
(the central subword of length
2n+1
in
x
).

For each
n
we have
q_{-n-1}\xrightarrow[a_{-n}]{b_{-n}}q_{-n}\dots\xrightarrow[a_{n}]{b_{n}}q_{n}
which is a run of
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q,Q,Q)
of length
2n+1
.
Hence
u_{n}\in{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}
. Therefore
x=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\triangleright^{k_{n}}(u_{n})
is in
\overrightarrow{{\mathcal{L}}_{{\mathcal{T}}}}

∎

C.3
Uniqueness of the Minimal Rooted Right-Resolving Pruned Presentation

We recall that a presentation of a sofic subshift
X
is simply a
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer with
{\mathds{1}}
for output alphabet, so we start by studying completeness in the case where
B={\mathds{1}}
. A presentation of a sofic subshift is:

Pruned

if its language is pruned, meaning that for every word
w
in the language, there exists
u
and
v
both non-empty such that
uwv
is in the language.

Right-Resolving

whenever its transition relation is a partial function.

Rooted

whenever there exists a root state
r
such that

•

every accepted word admits an accepted run starting by
r
,

•

and every state is accessible from
r
, meaning there is a run from
r
to that state.

Lemma C.7

(Pruned Presentation)
.

For any pruned presentation
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
of the sofic subshift
X
, the language recognized by the presentation is exactly
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
.

Proof.

Let
L
be the language recognized by
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
. By definition, we have
\overrightarrow{L}=X
.
By
Proposition

4.3
,
L=\mathrm{Fact}(\overrightarrow{L})=\mathrm{Fact}(X)
.
∎

Theorem C.8

(Uniqueness)
.

Every non-empty sofic subshift admits a unique minimal rooted right-resolving pruned presentation, up to isomorphism.

Proof.

Given a sofic subshift
X
, let us consider the minimal deterministic automaton
({\mathcal{D}},A,Q,\{i\},F)
that recognizes
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
. Within that automaton, we write
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{}
for the transitions, and
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w}
for the iterated transitions.

If we take
w=w_{1}\dots w_{n}\in\mathrm{Fact}(X)
, it corresponds to a run from
i\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w}f\in F
. Since
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
is factor-closed, we also have
w_{1}\dots w_{k}\in\mathrm{Fact}(X)
for
0\leq k\leq n
, and since
{\mathcal{D}}
is deterministic it means that all the states of the run
i\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w}f
are also final states. This means that if we restrict our automaton to only its final states, so
({\mathcal{D}},A,F,\{i\},F)
, then the language recognized is identical. Note that
{\mathcal{D}}
is now a partial function (except in the case
\mathrm{Fact}(X)=A^{*}
).

We now consider
q\in F
and
u\in A^{*}
such that there exists a
f\in F
satisfying
p\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{u}f
. In the minimal deterministic automaton, every state is accessible (otherwise the automaton would not be minimal), so there exists
v\in A^{*}
such that
i\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{v}q
, meaning that
vu\in\mathrm{Fact}(X)
. Since
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
is factor-closed, this means that
u\in\mathrm{Fact}(X)
, so there exists a run
i\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{v}p
for some
p\in F
. This means that if we extend initial states to be
F
, so the non-deterministic automaton
({\mathcal{D}},A,F,F,F)
, then the language recognized is identical. Additionally, any word accepted by this automaton admits an accepting run that start with
i
, and every state is accessible from
i
. Said otherwise,
({\mathcal{D}},A,F)
is a representation of the sofic subshift
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{Fact}(X)}
, that is pruned, right-resolving and rooted in
i
. Using
Proposition

4.3
,
\overrightarrow{\mathrm{Fact}(X)}=X
.

Now for uniqueness, we consider another minimal pruned right-resolving presentation
({\mathcal{M}},A,P)
, rooted in
r
. If
{\mathcal{M}}
is already total (which only happens whenever
\mathrm{Fact}(X)=A^{*}
),
({\mathcal{M}},A,P,\{r\},P)
is the minimal deterministic automaton recognizing
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
. Assuming
{\mathcal{M}}
is not a total function, we build build from it a deterministic automaton
({\mathcal{M}}_{\bot},A,P\sqcup\{\bot\},\{r\},P)
where
{\mathcal{M}}_{\bot}
is the total function that extends
{\mathcal{M}}
by adding a transition toward the new state
\bot
whenever
{\mathcal{M}}
would be undefined. This deterministic automaton recognizes the same language as the presentation, which according to
Lemma

C.7
is exactly
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
. We argue it is the minimal deterministic automaton recognizing
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
. Indeed, if we take a deterministic automaton recognizing
\mathrm{Fact}(X)
that is strictly smaller, then by following the same procedure we used above on
{\mathcal{D}}
to make it a pruned right-resolving presentation, we would obtain one that is strictly smaller than
{\mathcal{M}}
, which contradict minimality.

In both cases, we have a minimal deterministic automaton that recognizes the same language as
({\mathcal{D}},A,P,\{i\},F)
, so by uniqueness of the minimal automata (see
[
HMU07
]
) we have and isomorphism
\iota
between the two. This directly induces an isomorphism between
({\mathcal{D}},A,F)
and
({\mathcal{M}},A,P)
.
∎

Appendix D
Proof for Bi-Infinite Words

D.1
From
{\mathbb{Z}}
-Transducers to Graphical Representation

In this section, we prove
Proposition

4.1
, that is for every
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer
{\mathcal{T}}
, its behavior can be obtained using the lift of
{\mathcal{T}}
as follows:

{\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbb{Z}}
B^{\mathbb{Z}}
Q^{\mathbb{Z}}
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
Q^{\mathbb{Z}}
=
{\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
A^{\mathbb{Z}}
B^{\mathbb{Z}}

We write
{\mathcal{R}}
for the left-hand-side. We take
w\in A^{\mathbb{Z}}
and
v\in B^{\mathbb{Z}}
, and note that by definition of the composition of relations,
w\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ v
if and only there exists two words
q,u\in Q^{\mathbb{Z}}
such that
(w,q)\penalty\ {\mathcal{T}}^{\mathbb{Z}}\penalty\ (v,u)
and
q\penalty\ \left(\triangleright\right)^{t}\penalty\ u
.

Let us focus on the latter, it is equivalent to the for all
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
q_{k+1}=u_{k}
.
Let us focus on the former, it is equivalent to, for every
k\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
(w_{k},q_{k})\penalty\ {\mathcal{T}}\penalty\ (v_{k},u_{k})
, that is
q_{k}\xrightarrow[v_{k}]{w_{k}}u_{k}
. Combining both, we obtain
q_{k}\xrightarrow[v_{k}]{w_{k}}q_{k+1}
, which yields exactly the definition of a run. So we have that
w\penalty\ {\mathcal{R}}\penalty\ v
if and only if
w\penalty\ {\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)\penalty\ v
. ∎

Let us point a couple of facts about this language:

•

A bundle of wires labeled
A_{1},\dots,A_{n}
is the same as a single wire labeled
\Pi_{i=1}^{n}A_{i}
, and wires labeled
{\mathds{1}}
can be omitted.

•

The wires and every syntactical construct are in
thick red
, to distinguish them from actual relations. In particular,
{\mathcal{R}}
refers to an actual relation while
R
refers to an element of our language.

•

Double-line boxes denote the generator, while single-line boxes denote any diagram potentially constituted of many generators, including feedbacks.

•

The arrows on the feedback are a reminder that those are not the same as the cup and cap of
Section

2
.

•

Within
Figure

20
, the gray lines correspond to bracketing, and similarly to
Figure

7
the overall consequences of those equations is that bracketing can be safely ignored.

•

The equations of
Figure

21
ensures that if a diagram does not contain any instance of the “feedback” generator, we can merge all the generators into a single double-line box.

•

The current equational theory is incomplete, additional equations will be added in
Figure

6
to obtain completeness.

D.3
Soundness of the Standard Equations

We now prove the soundness of the equational theory.
Soundness means that if one rewrites a diagram
R
into
S
using any of the listed equations, we still have
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}={{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{S}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
. The soundness of the equation of
Figure

19
follows immediately from the fact that
Rel
is a strict symmetric monoidal category, so we only need to look at the equations of
Figure

20
. The top-left and bottom-left equations also follow from the fact that
Rel
is a strict symmetric monoidal category. The top-right is sound because
\triangleright_{\mathds{1}}=\textbf{{id}}_{\mathds{1}}
, and the bottom-right is sound because
\triangleright_{C}\times\triangleright_{D}=\triangleright_{C\times D}
. ∎

Proof.

We start by using the first equation of

from right to left to push all the feedbacks at the bottom of the diagram. Then, we use the equations of

to merge all the non-feedback into a single box. Lastly, we use the last equation of

to merge all the feedbacks into a single feedback.
∎
