Figure 25:
Forward-Simulation Principle for Bi-Infinite Words.

We start by proving the following compactness result.
For any
{\mathbb{Z}}
-transducer
({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)
, we recall that by definition, we have
(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\penalty\ {\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)\penalty\ (b_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
if and only if there exists
(q_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
such that
\forall n\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
(a_{n},q_{n+1})\penalty\ {\mathcal{T}}\penalty\ (b_{n},q_{n})
.

Theorem D.3

(Compactness)
.

It is sufficient to only consider finite sequences, meaning that
(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\penalty\ {\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,Q)\penalty\ (b_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
if and only if there exists for all
N\leq M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
a finite sequence
(q_{n}^{N,M})_{N\leq n\leq M}
such that
\forall N\leq n<M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
(a_{n},q^{N,M}_{n+1})\penalty\ {\mathcal{T}}\penalty\ (b_{n},q^{N,M}_{n})
.

Proof.

The direct implication is trivial, as we simply take
q_{n}^{N,M}:=q_{n}
. For the indirect implication, it is in fact enough to only consider the finite sequences centered on zero, that is
(q_{n}^{-N,N})_{-N\leq n\leq N}
for
N\geq 0
, and ignore those that are not centered on zero. From them, we will define inductively
(q_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
. In order to do so, we add an additional induction hypothesis: there exists an infinite amount of
N\geq n
such that
\forall-n\leq k\leq n
,
q^{-N,N}_{k}=q_{k}
.

We start by arbitrarily providing a total order
\leq_{Q}
over the finite set
Q
.

Base Case
: We look at all the
q\in Q
and check whether there exists an infinite amount of
N\geq 0
such that
q^{-N,N}_{0}=q
. Since
Q
is finite, there must exists a
q
satisfying that condition. We take
q_{0}
to be the minimal
q
(for
\leq_{Q}
) satisfying that condition.

Inductive Case
: We assume that we have defined
q_{-n},\dots,q_{0},\dots,q_{n}
such that:

•

For all
-n\leq k<n
,
(a_{n},q_{n+1})\penalty\ {\mathcal{T}}\penalty\ (b_{n},q_{n})
.

•

There exists an infinite amount of
N\geq n
such that
\forall-n\leq k\leq n
,
q^{-N,N}_{k}=q_{k}
.

We look at all the pairs
(q_{\ominus},q_{\oplus})\in Q^{2}
and check whether there exists an infinite amount of
N\geq n+1
such that
q^{-N,N}_{-n-1}=q_{\ominus}
and
q^{-N,N}_{n+1}=q_{\oplus}
. Since
Q^{2}
is finite, there must exists a pair
(q_{\ominus},q_{\oplus})
satisfying that condition. We take
(q_{-n-1},q_{n+1})
to be the minimal pair (for the lexicographic order) satisfying that condition. By definition, both induction hypothesis are preserved.
∎

We start by proving the soundness of
Figure

24
with respect to
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\right\rrbracket}}
. We need to prove that if we consider
(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\penalty\ {\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{R}},A,B,C)\penalty\ (b_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
then
(a_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}\penalty\ {\mathcal{L}}^{\mathbb{Z}}({\mathcal{T}},A,B,D)\penalty\ (b_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
.

Focusing on the right-hand-side, and using the above
Theorem

D.3
, it is enough to show there exists for all
N\leq M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
a finite sequence
(d_{n}^{N,M})_{N\leq n\leq M}
such that
\forall N\leq n<M\in{\mathbb{Z}}
,
(a_{n},d^{N,M}_{n+1})\penalty\ {\mathcal{T}}\penalty\ (b_{n},d^{N,M}_{n})
. Or, in diagrammatic terms, it is enough to show that the following relation relates

.

D.8
Completeness of the Simulation Principle

The core idea of the proof is similar to the finite case, meaning we will determinize then minimize the presentation of our sofic subshift, except we have to start by pruning the states of our presentation that cannot be part of any bi-infinite path. Whenever we consider
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
to be a presentation of sofic subshift, we write
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{}
for the transitions within that presentation, and we write
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{w}
with
w
a finite word of size
k
for the iterated transition
\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{w_{1}}\dots\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{w_{k}}
.

Definition D.1

(Forward-Pruning)
.

Let
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
be a presentation of a sofic subshift. Its forward-pruning is the presentation
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q^{\to\infty})
, where
Q^{\to\infty}
is the restriction of
Q
to the states
q_{0}
that are the start of an infinite path
q_{0}\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{1}}q_{1}\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{2}}\dots
, or equivalently of a path of size at least
\textup{card}(Q)
as such a path necessarily loops, and where
{\mathcal{T}}
has been restricted to
A\times Q^{\to\infty}\to Q^{\to\infty}
.

Proposition D.4
.

For all presentation of a sofic subshift
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
, we have

{\mathcal{T}}
=
A
Q^{\to\infty}
=
{\mathcal{T}}
=
Q
A
Q
Q
\subseteq
\textup{card}(Q^{\to\infty})
{\mathcal{T}}
\cdots
{\mathcal{T}}
=
Q^{\to\infty}
Q
=
Q^{\to\infty}
Q
\subseteq
\textup{card}(Q)
{\mathcal{T}}
Q
\cdots
{\mathcal{T}}
Q
Q^{\to\infty}
Q^{\to\infty}
Q^{\to\infty}
Q^{\to\infty}

where
=:Q\to Q^{\to\infty}
is the usual “equality” relation.

Proof.

The first equation state that if
q_{0}
is the start of an infinite path, and
q_{-1}\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{0}}q_{0}
, then
q_{-1}
is also the start of an infinite path, which is true.

The second equation is trivially true as the right-hand-side simplifies to
\bullet_{Q^{\to\infty}}
, which is maximal for the inclusion.

The third equation state that if
q_{0}
is the start of a path of size
\textup{card}(Q)
, then it is the start of an infinite path, which is true because a path of size
\textup{card}(Q)
necessarily loops. ∎

Corollary D.5
.

For all presentation of a sofic subshift
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
, using
Figure

6
, we have

{\mathcal{T}}

=
{\mathcal{T}}

Definition D.2

(Backward-Pruning)
.

Let
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
be a presentation of a sofic subshift. Its backward-pruning is the presentation
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q^{\infty\to})
, where
Q^{\infty\to}
is the restriction of
Q
to the states
q_{0}
that are the end of an infinite path
\dots\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{-1}}q_{-1}\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{0}}q_{0}
, or equivalently of a path of size at least
\textup{card}(Q)
as such a path necessarily loops, and where
{\mathcal{T}}
has been restricted to
A\times Q^{\infty\to}\to Q^{\infty\to}
.

Proposition D.6
.

For all presentation of a sofic subshift
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
, we have

{\mathcal{T}}
=
A
Q^{\infty\to}
=

Q
Q

\textup{card}(Q^{\infty\to})
{\mathcal{T}}
Q^{\infty\to}
\cdots
{\mathcal{T}}
Q^{\infty\to}
=

\supseteq
\textup{card}(Q)
{\mathcal{T}}
Q
\cdots
{\mathcal{T}}

where
=:Q^{\infty\to}\to Q

Proof.

The first equation state that if
q_{0}

q_{0}\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{1}}q_{1}
, then
q_{1}
is also the end of an infinite path, which is true.

The third equation state that if

necessarily loops.
∎

Definition D.3

(Pruning)
.

Let
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
be a presentation of a sofic subshift. Its pruning is the presentation
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q^{\infty\to\infty})
, where
Q^{\infty\to\infty}
is the restriction of
Q
to the states
q_{0}
that are on a bi-infinite path
\dots\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{-1}}q_{-1}\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{0}}q_{0}\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{1}}q_{1}\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a_{2}}\dots
, or equivalently at the middle of a path of size at least
2\times\textup{card}(Q)
as such a path necessarily loops before and after
q_{0}
, and where
{\mathcal{T}}
has been restricted to
A\times Q^{\infty\to}\to Q^{\infty\to}
.

Lemma D.8
.

The pruning of a presentation is equal to the forward-pruning of its backward-pruning, or equivalently the backward pruning of its forward-pruning. The pruning of a representation is always a
pruned
representation.

Proof.

If a state
q_{0}
is on a bi-infinite path
(q_{n})_{n\in{\mathbb{Z}}}
, then all the states
q_{n}
are on an bi-infinite path, so none of them will be removed by the forward-pruning or backward-pruning, meaning that
Q^{\infty\to\infty}\subseteq(Q^{\to\infty})^{\infty\to}
. We then take
q_{0}\in(Q^{\to\infty})^{\infty\to}
, there is an infinite path
(q_{n})_{n\leq 0}
in
(Q^{\to\infty})^{\infty\to}
that ends on
q_{0}
, and there is an infinite path
(q_{n})_{n\geq 0}
in
Q^{\to\infty}
that starts with
q_{0}
. So
q_{0}\in Q^{\infty\to\infty}
, hence
Q^{\infty\to\infty}=(Q^{\to\infty})^{\infty\to}
. The same reasoning can be done to prove
Q^{\infty\to\infty}=(Q^{\infty\to})^{\to\infty}
.
∎

Definition D.4

(Determinization)
.

Let
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
be a pruned presentation of a
non-empty
sofic subshift. Its determinization is the
pruned right-resolving rooted
presentation
(\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}}),A,\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q))
.
More precisely, we start by recalling that
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}(Q)
is the set of all non-empty subsets of
Q
. Since our subshift is non-empty,
Q\in\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}(Q)
. We use
x,y
for elements of
Q
, and
X
,
Y
for elements of
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}(Q)
. Then, we define the partial function
12
12

12

We consider partial functions to be a special case of relations.

\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}}):A\times\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}(Q)\to\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}(Q)
as

\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}})(a,X)=\{y\in Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ \exists x\in X,\penalty\ x\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a}y\}\textbf{{ if }}\text{it is non-empty (undefined otherwise)}

We then consider the set
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
of subsets of
Q
accessible by iteration of that function, starting from
Q
. We can now restrict
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}})
to a function
A\mapsto\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)\to\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
. The root is simply
Q
, and every state is accessible from
Q
, by definition of
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
.

Proposition D.9
.

For all pruned presentation of a non-empty sofic subshift
({\mathcal{T}},A,Q)
, we have

\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}})
\ni
A

=

\ni

\cdots
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}})
\ni

\ni

{\mathcal{T}}

{\mathcal{T}}

\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)

where
\ni:\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)\to Q
is the usual “contains” relation, that is
X\ni x
whenever
x\in X
.

Proof.

In this proof, we write
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})
for the total function from
A\times{\mathcal{P}}(Q)\to{\mathcal{P}}(Q)
which extends
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}})
as below, and note that
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}})(a,X)
is defined if and only if
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})(a,X)\neq\varnothing
.

{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})(a,X)=\{y\in Q\penalty\ |\penalty\ \exists x\in X,\penalty\ x\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a}y\}

Using the logical reasoning as in
Section

2
, we can rewrite the first equation as the following. We are looking at
\forall a\in A,\penalty\ \forall X\in\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q),\penalty\ \forall y\in Q,\penalty\

\left(\exists x\in Q,\penalty\ (x\in X)\land(x\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a}y)\right)\penalty\ \Leftrightarrow\penalty\ \left(\exists Y\in\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q),\penalty\ (X\xrightarrow[\text{\small$\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}})$}]{a}Y)\land(y\in Y)\right)

We start by reformulating the right side of the equivalence, as
\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}({\mathcal{T}})
is a partial function, we can replace the
\exists
and obtain
({\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})(a,X)\neq\varnothing)\land(y\in\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}(a,X))
, which is equivalent to
y\in{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})(a,X)
. Then, using the definition of
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})
, we obtain that it is equivalent to
\exists x\in X,x\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{T}}$}]{a}y
, which is exactly the left side of the equivalence.

The second and third equations are trivially true as their left-hand-side simplifies to
\bullet_{\mathcal{P}_{\neq\varnothing}^{\textup{acc}}(Q)}
and
\bullet_{Q}
, which are maximal for the inclusion.
∎

Definition D.5

(Minimization)
.

Let
({\mathcal{D}},A,P)
be a
pruned right-resolving rooted
presentation of a sofic subshift, and we write
r
for its
13
13

13

While it is possible for that presentation to have multiple roots, choosing a different root have no influence on this minimization, as the
L_{w}
defined would be identical.
root. Its minimzation is the
right-resolving rooted
presentation
(L_{\mathcal{D}},A,L^{\neq\varnothing}_{A^{*}})
.
More precisely, we write
L_{w}=\{v\in A^{*}\mid\exists f\in P,r\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{wv}f\}
, and remark that whenever
L_{w}=L_{u}
, then for all
a\in A
we also have
L_{wa}=L_{ua}
. We then define
L^{\neq\varnothing}_{A^{*}}=\{L_{w}\neq\varnothing\mid w\in A^{*}\}
note that a single element of this set might correspond to multiple distinct
w
, and in fact
L_{A^{*}}
is actually smaller or equal to
P
in cardinality. Lastly, we define the transition function as
L_{\mathcal{D}}(a,L_{w})=L_{wa}
whenever it is non-empty, and undefined otherwise.

Similarly to the uniqueness of the minimal deterministic automata, this minimization yield the unique (up to isomorphism) minimal
pruned right-resolving rooted
presentation of the given sofic subshift, see
Theorem

C.8
.

.

For all pruned right-resolving rooted presentation

to

whenever

. We note that since every state is accessible, there exists a
w\in A^{*}
such that the later is equal to
L_{w}
.

Proof.

Using the logical reasoning as in
Section

2
, we can rewrite the first equation as the following. We are looking at
\forall a\in A,\forall p\in P,\forall\ell\in L^{\neq\varnothing}_{A^{*}},

\begin{array}[]{c}\left(\exists q\in P,\penalty\ (p\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{a}q)\land(\ell=\{w\mid\exists f\in F,q\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w}f\})\right)\\
\Leftrightarrow\\
\left(\exists m\in L^{\neq\varnothing}_{A^{*}},\penalty\ (m=\{v\mid\exists f\in P,p\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{v}f\})\land(m\xrightarrow[\text{\small$L_{\mathcal{D}}$}]{a}\ell)\right)\end{array}

Before studying either side of that equivalence, we note that since
p
is accessible from the root state
r
, there exists a
u\in A^{*}
such that
r\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{u}p
, hence
L_{u}=\{v\mid\exists f\in F,p\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{v}f\}
.

We start by reformulating the second part of the equivalence. Since the formula for
m
is given, we can remove the
\exists m
and obtain
L_{u}\xrightarrow[\text{\small$L_{\mathcal{D}}$}]{a}\ell
. We can now use the definition of
L_{\mathcal{D}}
and simplify the later in
(L_{ua}\neq\varnothing)\land(\ell=L_{ua})
.

We now look at the first part of the equivalence, since
{\mathcal{D}}
is right-resolving,
q
is uniquely determined if it exists, so we can remove
\exists q
and we obtain
({\mathcal{D}}(a,p)\text{ is defined})\land(\ell=\{w\mid\exists f\in F,{\mathcal{D}}(a,p)\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{w}f\})
. Since
r\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{u}p
, this is equivalent to
(L_{ua}\neq\varnothing)\land(\ell=\{w\mid\exists f\in F,r\xrightarrow[\text{\small${\mathcal{D}}$}]{uaw}f\})
, that is
(L_{ua}\neq\varnothing)\land(\ell=L_{ua})
.

The second and third equations are trivially true as their right-hand-side simplifies to
\bullet_{L_{A^{*}}}
and
\bullet_{P}
, which are maximal for the inclusion.
∎

Then, we use
Corollaries

D.5
and
D.7
on
{\mathcal{T}}
to prune it, and we distinguish two cases.

Case 1
: If
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
is the empty relation, then the result of the pruning is empty, meaning that:

By combining with the cap
\eta_{B}
to bend the input
B
into an output, and using the the fact that
(\textbf{{id}}_{B}\times\epsilon_{B})\circ(\eta_{B}\times\textbf{{id}}_{B})=\textbf{{id}}_{B}
, we obtain
{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{R}}}}}={{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{S}}}}}
.

Case 2
: If
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
is non-empty, then the result of the pruning is non-empty. We can continue and use
Corollary

D.10
on
{\mathcal{T}}
followed by
Corollary

D.12
on
{\mathcal{P}}({\mathcal{T}})
to obtain:

For convenience, we name
({\mathcal{R}}_{M},B\times A,Q_{M})
the resulting minimal representation. We do the same for
{\mathcal{S}}
and write
({\mathcal{S}}_{N},B\times A,Q_{N})
for the resulting minimal representation. Since
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{R}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}={{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{S}}}}}}\right\rrbracket}}
and by soundness of the equations, we obtain that:

Using the definition of
{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\left\llbracket{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}-}\right\rrbracket}}
and
Proposition

4.1
, it follows that
({\mathcal{R}}_{M},B\times A,Q_{M})
and
({\mathcal{S}}_{N},B\times A,Q_{N})
represent the same subshift, hence by uniqueness of the minimal (pruned right-resolving rooted) representation of
Theorem

C.8
, we obtain that both representations are equal up to an isomorphism
\iota:Q_{M}\to Q_{N}
, hence using the simulation principle with this
\iota
, we obtain

\hss}\lxSVG@closescope \hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin=1} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width=0.4pt} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}

By combining with the cap
\eta_{B}
to bend the input
B
into an output, and using the the fact that
(\textbf{{id}}_{B}\times\epsilon_{B})\circ(\eta_{B}\times\textbf{{id}}_{B})=\textbf{{id}}_{B}
, we obtain
{{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{R}}}}}={{{\color[rgb]{0.5,0,0}\textbf{{S}}}}}
.
∎
